<#sep#> with their own public institutions, chiefly in villages. Several dozen yeshivas, with thousands of students, became important Torah centers. Slovakian Jews played a significant economic role as mediators between the agricultural sector and the growing cities and were involved in trade in agricultural produce. They included merchants, tenant farmers and estate managers, but many worked in petty commerce as artisans or peddlers occupations that Jews in the Oberland had practiced for generations.
Whereas manifestations of socioeconomically motivated anti-Semitism that had erupted in the early nineteenth century declined when the revolution of 1848-1849 was suppressed, nationally based anti-Semitism increased over time. The Slovakian intelligentsia, which advocated a Slovakian national revival, regarded the Jews as tools of the hated Hungarian regime and, therefore, one of the causes of the plight of the Slovakian people. After Hungarian Jewry attained equal rights in 1867, anti-Semitic activity increased in Slovakia. Church representatives were actively involved in stirring up hatred of the Jews. In July 1882 some two hundred priests gathered in Topolcany to discuss the "Jewish question". The upshot of their deliberations was to urge the Hungarian parliament to revoke or limit the equal rights that it had granted to Jews in 1867.
From the beginning of the eighteenth century the empire had been undergoing a modernization process that was paralleled by similar changes in Hungarian Jewry. The ideas of the Enlightenment that seeped into Hungary during those years influenced many Hungarian Jews to cast aside their traditions and seek their future in European culture. Hungarian liberals encouraged Jews to abandon their traditional lifestyle and adopt the customs of enlightened Hungarian society. Attempts to amend or reform religious rules were met with vigorous opposition from both the religious leadership in Slovakia and a large portion of the Jewish population, which was characterized by a conservative way of life and a strong affinity for religion and tradition. The opposition to the reform trends was led, from the very beginning, by Rabbi Moshe Sofer of Pressburg. As a result of his vehement stance Pressburg became the center of the struggle against the Haskala (Jewish Enlightenment) movement and religious reforms. In the mid-nineteenth century the Kulturkampf within Hungarian Jewry intensified; and in 1865 dozens of Orthodox rabbis gathered in Michalovce, Slovakia. They adopted stringent resolutions including a boycott of synagogues that had instituted changes, and a strict injunction against sermons in German and Hungarian, secular education, and the study of foreign languages. The resolutions were given the force of a halakhic ruling, thereby further deepening the fissure among Hungarian Jewry and precluding all attempts to heal the rift. At the initiative of the authorities, representatives of Hungarian Jewry were invited to Budapest in December 1868 to promote the emancipation and to establish a countrywide umbrella organization for all Jewish communities in the country. The Liberal Jews welcomed the initiative, but the Orthodox regarded it with great suspicion. They feared that such an organization, supported by the authorities and the Liberal Jews, would diminish the communities' independence and undermine the status of the rabbi as supreme religious authority and sole arbiter. After all attempts at compromise failed, the Orthodox delegates walked out of the congress. The conflict between the camps reached its peak, causing a rupture between the communities and a deep polarization of Hungarian Jewry. As a result, two separate organizations of communities were established one for the Orthodox and one for the Liberals (Neologs). Some communities did not join either organization and retained their previous status; these communities were known as status quo ante. The Jews of Slovakia were more united in their affinity for religion than those in the rest of Hungary. Two-thirds of the communities, especially the old, traditional ones in the small towns and villages, joined the Orthodox organization. In parallel, most new communities, primarily in central Slovakia, joined the Neologs. In several locations two communities formed, one Orthodox and one Neolog, and they vied for control of community institutions and assets that had previously belonged to the joint administration.
On the eve of World War I the Jewish population of Slovakia numbered 140,000. Collectively they were quite diverse, comprising several religious factions whose members differed in their way of life, affinity for religion, origins, and cultural backgrounds. They spoke four different languages among themselves and with the Christians, belonged to various socioeconomic classes, and were divided among national groups. Most Slovakian Jews originated in villages and small towns and had traits characteristic of post-rural society.
After World War I social agitation grew in Slovakia. Economic hardship
increased following the return and demobilization of soldiers, and the
frustration was soon vented in several weeks of violent acts against Jews. The
burglary and looting of homes and businesses affected Jews of all classes, poor
and rich alike.
The new government adopted the Habsburg monarchies legislation regarding the Jews: namely, in the Czech province the Austrian laws remained in effect, whereas in Slovakia the Hungarian laws remained but with slight modifications. Attempts to establish a countrywide organization of communities representing all Jews in Czechoslovakia were foiled by the Orthodox leadership in Slovakia, which feared that this would undermine its hegemony among the religious Jews. Meanwhile, a new source of friction further exacerbated the tense relations between the factions. Under the Czechoslovakian constitution members of the Jewish religion could declare that they belonged to the Jewish nation. The Orthodox were vehemently opposed to defining Judaism as a national entity. To them Jewry was solely a religious community and would remain so until the Jews were redeemed from exile. Nevertheless, Jewish nationalism, represented by the Zionist movement, managed to establish itself and make inroads even among traditional Jews. Many Zionists, especially the young, came from Orthodox families; they joined Jewish national and Zionist organizations despite the opposition of the Orthodox religious leadership.
After Czechoslovakia was established the organizations of Jewish communities formed their institutions. The Organization of Autonomous Orthodox Congregations in Slovakia (OAOCS) included 170 of 228 congregations and about 75 percent of Slovakian Jews. Over time it became one of the most authoritative, powerful and influential Jewish organizations in Czechoslovakia. Meanwhile, Agudath Israel, which had close ties to the OAOCS, complemented it by carrying out a wide range of activities in dozens of branches. Agudath Israel focused primarily on individual life and on education consistent with Orthodoxy. The ties between the two organizations, strong to begin with, became even stronger when Rabbi Samuel David Ungar, one of the leaders of the OAOCS, was named president of Agudath Israel in Czechoslovakia. Agudath Israel and its youth movements engaged in ramified social and welfare activities and educational initiatives, including preschools, a Beth Yaakov school system for girls, and camps for children and teenagers. At the end of World War I there were twenty-nine Neolog communities in Slovakia. Cut off from their center in Hungary, they encountered complex organizational problems that threatened their survival. In 1925 the Neologs decided to form a joint organization with the fifty-five Status Quo Ante communities, which since 1928 had been known as Jeshurun.
In 1920 there were seventy-seven Jewish primary schools in Slovakia and two high schools. Forty-six of the schools were Orthodox; the rest were liberal. Noteworthy, only 45 percent of Jewish children attended Jewish schools; the majority attended public schools. Due to the growing percentage of students in schools that taught in Slovakian, the language spoken by the young Jews changed. Instead of German and Hungarian, the main languages used by Slovakian Jews in the past, the young people adopted Slovakian as their vernacular. Alongside the formal educational system, an extensive system of Torah education institutions functioned in Slovakia, including boys' schools (cheder and Talmud Torah), Beth Yaacov seminaries for religious girls, and more than thirty yeshivas that were accredited as religious educational institutions. In 1930 the Pressburg (Bratislava) yeshiva and three other yeshivas were accredited by the Czechoslovakian Ministry of Education as institutions of higher education.
Vibrant Zionist activity was enjoyed in inter-war Slovakia and new branches of the movement were established in dozens of cities and towns. In addition to the Zionist Organization, there was a Jewish National Party (Zidovska Strana) that focused on domestic Jewish policy and representation of the Jews national, religious, economic, and social interests vis-a-vis the authorities. The party did well in elections for town councils and county and regional assemblies. Although the Orthodox establishment doggedly fought Zionism and the Jewish National Party, the Zionists penetrated even the Orthodox communities. In the mid-1930s about 20 percent of Slovakian Jews purchased the Zionist "Shekel", thereby becoming formal members of the Zionist Movement.
Zionist youth movements predated World War I. In 1919-1920, after the wartime turmoil in Slovakia had waned, Zionist youth groups reorganized and opened clubs in all the major cities and some small towns. In 1924, representatives of Zionist youth groups met in Nitra and together founded He-haluts. Religious teenagers established Mizrachi Youth. Organizational and<#sep#>-

mod_val = 10**9 + 7
mod_len = mod_val - 1

val = list(range(10))
length = [1] * 10

s = input().strip()
n = int(input())
queries = [input().strip().replace('->', ' ').split() for _ in range(n)]

for di_str, ti in reversed(queries):
    di = int(di_str)
    new_val = 0
    current_power = 0
    new_len = 0
    for c in reversed(ti):
        d = int(c)
        new_val = (new_val + val[d] * pow(10, current_power, mod_val)) % mod_val
        current_power = (current_power + length[d]) % mod_len
        new_len = (new_len + length[d]) % mod_len
    val[di] = new_val
    length[di] = new_len

for c in s:
    d = int(c)

So where is the mistake here?

Wait, the problem says "find the maximum value of e". If there's no upper bound, then the answer would be infinity. But since it's a math competition problem, likely the answer is 5, but our previous example shows that e can be 9, 41, etc., so maybe the problem has constraints that variables are positive integers? Let me check the original problem again.

The problem states: "Given the equation a + b + c + d + e = abcde, find the maximum value of e."

No constraints are given. So unless otherwise specified, variables can be real numbers. So if variables can be real numbers, then as shown, e can be made arbitrarily large by taking d approaching 1 from above. But that would imply that there's no maximum value. However, the problem asks to find the maximum value, so this suggests that there might be constraints assumed, like variables being positive integers.

If we assume variables are positive integers, then the previous example where a=b=c=1, d=2, e=5 works. Let's check if there are integer solutions with larger e.

Assume a,b,c,d are positive integers ≥1.

Let's set a=b=c=1, d=1. Then equation is4 +e= e→4=0, impossible.

a=b=c=1, d=2: sum=5 +e, product=2e→e=5.

a=b=c=1, d=3: sum=6 +e, product=3e→e=3.

a=b=c=1, d=4: sum=7 +e, product=4e→7 +e=4e→e=7/3≈2.333, not integer.

So e=5 is maximum here. If we try setting a=1, b=1, c=2, d=2: sum=1+1+2+2 +e=6 +e; product=4e→6 +e=4e→e=2.

Alternatively, a=1, b=1, c=1, d=5: sum=8 +e, product=5e→e=8/(5-1)=2. Not larger than 5.

Alternatively, set a=1, b=1, c=1, d=1. Then impossible.

Alternatively, set a=1, b=1, c=2, d=3: sum=7 +e, product=6e→e=7/(6-1)=7/5=1.4, not integer.

Alternatively, set a=2, b=2, c=2, d=2: sum=8 +e, product=16e→e=8/(16-1)=8/15≈0.533, not integer.

If we set a=1, b=1, c=1, d=4, e=7/3: not integer.

So with integer variables, the maximum e is 5. However, the problem doesn't specify variables must be integers. If variables are allowed to be real numbers, then as per earlier examples, e can be made arbitrarily large. But since the problem asks for the maximum value, it's possible that the intended answer is 5 under integer assumptions.

Alternatively, maybe there's a different approach where variables are positive reals. Let's see.

Assume all variables are positive real numbers. Let's consider using the AM–GM inequality.

The equation is a + b + c + d + e = abcde.

Let’s denote S = a + b + c + d + e = abcde.

By AM ≥ GM,

S/5 ≥ (abcde)^(1/5).

But since S = abcde,

(abcde)/5 ≥ (abcde)^(1/5).

Let’s set t = (abcde)^(1/5). Then,

t^5 /5 ≥ t → t^4 ≥5 → t ≥ 5^(1/4).

Therefore, abcde ≥ 5^(5/4) ≈7.523. But this is a lower bound on the product, which is equal to the sum S. However, this doesn't directly help in finding the maximum e.

Alternatively, we can use Lagrange multipliers to maximize e subject to the constraint a + b + c + d + e = abcde.

Set up the Lagrangian: L = e + λ(abcde - a - b - c - d - e).

Take partial derivatives:

∂L/∂a = λ(bcde) - λ =0 → bcde =1

Similarly, ∂L/∂b = λ(acde) -λ=0 →acde=1

Similarly, ∂L/∂c=abde=1

∂L/∂d=abce=1

∂L/∂e=1 +λ(abcd) -λ=0 →λ(abcd -1) =-1.

From the first four equations:

From bcde=1, acde=1, abde=1, abce=1.

Divide the first equation by the second: (bcde)/(acde)=1/1 →b/a=1→a=b.

Similarly, divide first by third: (bcde)/(abde)=1/1 →c/a=1→a=c.

Similarly, divide first by fourth: (bcde)/(abce)=1/1 →d/a=1→a=d.

Thus, a=b=c=d. Let’s set a=b=c=d=x. Then the constraint equation becomes 4x + e =x^4 e. Solving for e: e( x^4 -1)=4x →e=4x/(x^4 -1).

Now, we need to maximize e=4x/(x^4 -1) for x>1 (since denominator must be positive; if x<1, denominator is negative, e negative, but since we are maximizing, we can consider x>1).

Take derivative of e with respect to x:

de/dx = [4(x^4 -1) -4x(4x^3)]/(x^4 -1)^2 = [4x^4 -4 -16x^4]/(x^4 -1)^2 = [-12x^4 -4]/(x^4 -1)^2.

The numerator is always negative for x>1, so de/dx <0. Hence, e is decreasing for x>1. Therefore, the maximum e occurs at the minimal x>1. As x approaches 1 from the right, e approaches 4x/(x^4 -1) → 4*1/(1 -1)→infinity.

Thus, under the assumption that variables are positive reals, e can be made arbitrarily large, hence no maximum. But this contradicts the problem statement, which asks for the maximum value of e. Therefore, this suggests that either there's a constraint missing, or there's a different approach.

Wait, but in the Lagrangian method, we found that a=b=c=d. So according to this, the maximum occurs when a=b=c=d=x and e=4x/(x^4 -1), which can be increased without bound as x approaches 1. But this contradicts the problem's expectation of a finite maximum. Therefore, maybe the problem assumes that all variables are positive integers, in which case the maximum e is 5. But the problem didn't specify this. Alternatively, maybe there's another constraint.

Alternatively, perhaps I'm missing something. When we used the Lagrangian multiplier method, we assumed that variables are positive, but what if variables can be negative? Would that allow for a finite maximum e?

Let’s consider variables can be negative. Suppose three variables are negative and two positive. Let's set a,b,c negative, and d,e positive. Then product abcde is negative (since three negatives), and the sum a + b + c + d + e could be positive or negative. If we want e to be maximum (positive), perhaps the product is negative, so the sum would have to be negative. But e is positive and possibly large. Hmmm.

Alternatively, let's consider two variables negative and three positive. The product would be positive (two negatives) if three positive variables. Then the sum could be positive. Let's try. For example, set a = -1, b = -1, c = d =1. Then equation is -1 -1 +1 +1 +e = (-1)(-1)(1)(1)e →0 +e=1*e→e=e. Which is always true, so e can be any value. But that's not helpful. So e can be any value here, which is problematic. Therefore, this approach isn't helpful.

Alternatively, set a = -2, b = -1, c = d =1. Then equation is -2 -1 +1 +1 +e = (-<#sep#> = f(k, memo)
        fy = f(n -k, memo)
        for a in fk:
            for b in fy:
                ... etc.

Thus the code:

import math as m;memo={1:{(1,1)}}

def f(n, memo=memo):
    if n in memo: return memo[n]
    s = set()
    for k in range(1, n):
        fk = f(k, memo)
        fy = f(n -k, memo)
        for a in fk:
            for b in fy:
                # series
                S = (a[0]*b[1]+a[1]*b[0], a[1]*b[1])
                g = m.gcd(*S)
                s.add( (S[0]//g, S[1]//g) )
                # parallel
                P = a[0]*b[0], a[0]*b[1] + a[1]*b[0]
                h = m.gcd(*P)
                s.add( (P[0]//h, P[1]//h) )
    memo[n] = s
    return s

In the mid-1930s about 20 percent of Slovakian Jews purchased the Zionist "Shekel", thereby becoming formal members of the Zionist Movement.
Zionist youth movements predated World War I. In 1919-1920, after the wartime turmoil in Slovakia had waned, Zionist youth groups reorganized and opened clubs in all the major cities and some small towns. In 1924, representatives of Zionist youth groups met in Nitra and together founded He-haluts. Religious teenagers established Mizrachi Youth. Organizational and social activity in the Zionist youth movements reached its peak in the 1930s, with tens of thousands of young members from all strata of Jewish society. Hundreds of them emigrated to Palestine after undergoing training for this purpose in Slovakia. The Maccabi sports association launched social and cultural activity with Zionist national leanings. Thousands of youngsters and adults, Orthodox and Liberal alike, were active in clubs throughout Slovakia.
During the period of the Republic of Czechoslovakia, Slovakian Jewry did not undergo any major demographic changes. In the 1930s about 140,000 Jews lived in 2,337 localities. Some 55 percent of them lived in villages and small towns with populations of less than 5,000; 18 percent lived in the two largest cities (Bratislava and Kosice), and only 25 percent lived in cities of l0,000-25,000 residents. The socioeconomic status of Slovakian Jews also remained constant during this period: 72 percent of Jewish breadwinners were self-employed, 15 percent were wage earners, and 13 percent were practitioners of liberal professions, white-collar workers, and brokers.
In the second half of the 1930s, as political tensions mounted in
Czechoslovakia, anti-Semitic sentiments increased among large segments of the
Slovakian people. The nationalist parties that agitated for Slovakian national
autonomy, especially the Slovak Peoples Party, held strong anti-Semitic
positions and incited against the Jews. On October 6, 1938, after the Munich
agreement in late September had forced Czechoslovakia to cede territory to the
Third Reich, the Slovak Peoples Party declared extensive autonomy in Slovakia
and instituted a one-party totalitarian regime. On November 2 large portions of
southern Slovakia home to more than 45,000 Jews were annexed to
Hungary. The Slovakian government blamed the Jews regarding their supposed
support of the annexation and started removing thousands of Jewish families who
held foreign citizenship to the Hungarian and Polish borders. Few of the
deportees were permitted to return to their homes.
While the deportations were in progress, an underground cell known as the Working Group, headed by Rabbi Michael Dov Weissmandel and Gisi Fleischmann, formed within the Center of Jews in an attempt to stanch the deportations and aid the deportees. The Working Groups success in halting the deportations from Slovakia encouraged its members to intensify their efforts to save all of European Jewry by negotiating with the Nazis. The resulting initiative, devised by Rabbi Weissmandel, was known as the Europa Plan. In April 1944 two Slovakian Jews, Alfred Wetzler and Walter Rosenberg (Rudolf Vrba), escaped from Auschwitz. The Working Group took detailed testimony from them about the nature of the Auschwitz camp and the extermination methods used there. A sketch of the extermination facilities, based on the description given by the two men, was attached to their testimony. The Auschwitz Protocols, as the document was known, were sent from Slovakia to the free world, where they reached Jewish organizations. The Working Groups purpose in releasing the Protocols was to sound an alarm and marshal worldwide public opinion in favor of extensive rescue activities and bombing of the railroads leading to Auschwitz and the extermination facilities themselves.
Jews joined the first resistance groups that organized in Slovakia in 1942. Most of them were active in the Communist and Czechoslovakian underground movements. A Jewish underground group was also formed in the Novaky labor camp in 1942. These groups tried to prevent a resumption of the deportations and started preparing for active resistance. On August 29, 1944, an armed uprising broke out in Slovakia in an attempt to overthrow the pro-Nazi regime and reestablish the Republic of Czechoslovakia. As the partisan attacks intensified, the German army invaded Slovakia. The Jewish group from the Novaky camp was assigned to halt the advance of the SS troops along one of their main attack routes. The German invasion of Slovakia augured ill for the last remaining Jews. Many tried to reach the rebel-controlled area in the hope of surviving, and thousands of destitute Jewish refugees gathered in the rebels main stronghold, Banska Bystrica. Parachutists sent by the Yishuv (the pre-Israel Jewish community in Palestine), headed by Haviva Reik, mobilized to help them. Although the uprising was quelled two months after it began, rebel units, including Jews, fled to the mountains, where they continued to engage the enemy until the liberation. About 1,600 Jews fought in various partisan units; approximately 170 of them were killed.
After the uprising was suppressed, the Germans took over the authority for Jewish affairs. SS officer Alois Brunner, one of Eichmanns assistants, went to Slovakia to deport all Jews irrespective of their status or their "certificates of exemption". Those Jews who were captured by the Nazis and their Slovakian accomplices were taken to the Sered camp. The deportation of the remaining Jews in Slovakia resumed on September 30, 1944. From then until March 31, 1945, some 12,000 Jews were deported from Slovakia; only half survived. Another 2,500 Jews were murdered on Slovakian soil during this period. Additional victims among Slovakian Jews were those who had fled to Hungary and were deported from there to the extermination camps.
The Jews in the territories annexed to Hungary in 1938-1939 met the same fate
as those in the rest of Hungary. After the annexation of these territories,
Hungarians began to persecute the Jews and accused them of supporting
Czechoslovakia. Of the 10,600 business owners only 4,500 were permitted to keep
their establishments going. Beginning in 1940, close to 7,500 men from southern
Slovakia were taken to work in labor battalions; few survived. Several thousand
Jews lacking Hungarian citizenship were deported in 1941 to the occupied part
of Ukraine, where most of them were murdered. After the Germans occupied
Hungary (March 19, 1944), new anti-Jewish edicts were promulgated.
Ghettoization of the Jews began in the second half of April 1944. The first
transports from the territories annexed to Hungary left for Auschwitz in the
second half of May 1944; the rest of the deportations occurred in June of that
year. Of some 45,000 Jews who lived in those territories, 10,000 survived.
About 100,000 Slovakian Jews 73 percent of their number in 1938
perished during World War II.

import sys
from collections import deque, defaultdict

n = int(sys.stdin.readline())
names = [sys.stdin.readline().strip() for _ in range(n)]

adj = defaultdict(set)
in_degree = {chr(ord('a') + i): 0 for i in range(26)}

for i in range(n-1):
    a = names[i]
    b = names[i+1]
    min_len = min(len(a), len(b))
    found = False
    for j in range(min_len):
        if a[j] != b[j]:
            c1 = a[j]
            c2 = b[j]
            if c2 not in adj[c1]:
                adj[c1].add(c2)
                in_degree[c2] += 1
            found = True
            break
    if not found:
        if len(a) > len(b):
            print("Impossible")
            exit()

queue = deque()
for c in in_degree:
    if in_degree[c] == 0:
        queue.append(c)

result = []
while queue:
    u = queue.popleft()
    result.append(u)
    for v in adj[u]:
        in_degree[v] -= 1
        if in_degree[v] == 0:
            queue.append(v)

if len(result) != 26:
    print("Impossible")
else:
    print(''.join(result))<#sep#>**Employee Severance Expenses**: What was the total amount recorded for employee severance expenses during the three months ended December 31, 2022, and how were these expenses categorized in the financial statements?<#sep#>. For example, set a = -1, b = -1, c = d =1. Then equation is -1 -1 +1 +1 +e = (-1)(-1)(1)(1)e →0 +e=1*e→e=e. Which is always true, so e can be any value. But that's not helpful. So e can be any value here, which is problematic. Therefore, this approach isn't helpful.

Alternatively, set a = -2, b = -1, c = d =1. Then equation is -2 -1 +1 +1 +e = (-2)(-1)(1)(1)e →(-1) +e=2e→-1=e. So e=-1. But we're looking for maximum e, which is positive, so this isn't useful.

Alternatively, set a= -k, b= -k, c= d=1, then equation is -k -k +1 +1 +e = (-k)(-k)(1)(1)e →(-2k +2 +e)=k^2 e. So solving for e: e + (-2k +2) =k^2 e → (-2k +2)=e(k^2 -1) →e= ( -2k +2 ) / (k^2 -1 )= (-2(k -1))/((k-1)(k +1))= -2/(k +1). So e= -2/(k +1). Since we want e positive, denominator must be negative. So k +1 <0 →k < -1. But k was defined as positive (since a=-k). Therefore, this isn't possible. So e is negative.

Hmm, this seems tricky. Maybe using negative variables doesn't help in increasing e beyond what's possible with positive variables.

Therefore, returning, if variables are allowed to be real numbers, there's no maximum e. But the problem likely expects an answer, so perhaps the intended answer is 5, assuming variables are positive integers. Given that in the integer case, e=5 is achievable, and that's the maximum possible. Therefore, the answer is 5.
</think>To find the maximum value of \(e\) in the equation \(a + b + c + d + e = abcde\), we will consider the case where \(a, b, c, d, e\) are positive integers.

1. **Setting \(a = b = c = 1\)**:
   Let's set \(a = b = c = 1\) and \(d\) as a variable. The equation simplifies to:
   \[
   1 + 1 + 1 + d + e = 1 \cdot 1 \cdot 1 \cdot d \cdot e
   \]
   This simplifies to:
   \[
   3 + d + e = d e
   \]
   Rearranging terms, we get:
   \[
   e(d - 1) = 3 + d
   \]
   Solving for \(e\), we obtain:
   \[
   e = \frac{3 + d}{d - 1}
   \]

2. **Finding the maximum \(e\)**:
   To maximize \(e\), we need to find the value of \(d\) that maximizes \(\frac{3 + d}{d - 1}\). We can test different integer values of \(d\):

   - For \(d = 2\):
     \[
     e = \frac{3 + 2}{2 - 1} = \frac{5}{1} = 5
     \]
     The equation becomes:
     \[
     1 + 1 + 1 + 2 + 5 = 1 \cdot 1 \cdot 1 \cdot 2 \cdot 5
     \]
     Simplifying both sides:
     \[
     10 = 10
     \]
     This is valid.

   - For \(d = 3\):
     \[
     e = \frac{3 + 3}{3 - 1} = \frac{6}{2} = 3
     \]
     The equation becomes:
     \[
     1 + 1 + 1 + 3 + 3 = 1 \cdot 1 \cdot 1 \cdot 3 \cdot 3
     \]
     Simplifying both sides:
     \[
     9 = 9
     \]
     This is valid, but \(e = 3\) is smaller than \(e = 5\).

   - For \(d = 4\):
     \[
     e = \frac{3 + 4}{4 - 1} = \frac{7}{3} \approx 2.333
     \]
     Since \(e\) must be an integer, this is not a valid solution.

   - For \(d = 5\):
     \[
     e = \frac{3 + 5}{5 - 1} = \frac{8}{4} = 2
     \]
     The equation becomes:
     \[
     1 + 1 + 1 + 5 + 2 = 1 \cdot 1 \cdot 1 \cdot 5 \cdot 2
     \]
     Simplifying both sides:
     \[
     10 = 10
     \]
     This is valid, but \(e = 2\) is smaller than \(e = 5\).

   - For \(d > 5\):
     As \(d\) increases, \(\frac{3 + d}{d - 1}\) decreases, resulting in smaller values of \(e\).

3. **Conclusion**:
   The maximum value of \(e\) occurs when \(d = 2\), giving \(e = 5\).

Thus, the maximum value of \(e\) is:
\[
\boxed{5}
\]<#sep#> m o = { 1 : { ( 1 , 1 ) } }

Since the eruption of the sub-prime mortgage crises of 2007 to 2009, there have been hot debates on how the current “greedy capitalism” must change into sustainable capitalism.
The “Occupy Wall Street” protests were against the far-reaching negative consequences of neo-liberal capitalism.
In the wake of the global financial meltdown, the notion of Capitalism 4.0 has suddenly made headlines and the Davos Forum early this year also addressed how to make capitalism sustainable.
To what extent is this debate and the search for a new version of capitalism relevant to Korea?
Unlike advanced Western capitalist states with more than 300 years of history, Korea’s modern development of capitalism occurred in the past half century but is regarded as the representative economy of East Asian miracle performers.
Korea formally adopted capitalism in the post-liberation era after 1945 by formulating a constitution containing a market economy as its basic tenet to promote economic freedom and encourage creativity.
As a corollary of this fundamental principle, the Korean constitution dictates that the government can regulate and adjust economic matters to supplement market economic principles to achieve social justice and economic democracy.
Empirical analysis suggests that the Korean economy has achieved both growth and equity.
Ironically, at the outset of Korea’s modern economic development, the Korean War (1950-1953) allowed everyone in Korea to be on equal footing.
Furthermore, Korea, with the birth of a new democratic government, carried out two institutional reforms: land reform based on owner-cultivator principles and a compulsory education system for school-age children to be enrolled at a primary school, at the very least.
In the past half century, Korea’s rapid economic growth has evolved around the creation of chaebol (large conglomerates), administered credit allocation with easy monetary policies from banks to priority sectors and export-oriented outward-looking policies to concentrate economic power highly in favor of them.
However, in the post-1997/98 Asian financial crisis era, and with the recent global crisis, Korea’s compressed growth model has resulted in worsening income polarization and jobless growth.
Korea now faces serious challenges on its way to introducing a welfare state paradigm. As part of the campaigns for the general elections concluded last week, left-wing opposition parties introduced a series of free programs involving everything from school lunches, school tuition, universal welfare and college tuition reduction to tax hikes for the rich and a chaebol tax.
The conservative government party also put forward its own welfare policy platforms largely based on selective and prudential principles.
What type of welfare state Korea should design is likely to be a key issue in the December presidential election.
Korea should consider a welfare state as a distinct combination of democracy, welfare and capitalism, as implemented with different degrees of emphasis by Germany, all the Nordic countries, the Netherlands, New Zealand and the United Kingdom.
Given Korea’s transition to a matured advanced economy and ongoing income polarization, the country must address how to balance growth dynamism and equity.
Above all, the government should do its best to construct a social safety net for those in need.
Without due consideration of fiscal sustainability, we should avoid the popular approach to embrace “something for nothing.”
Korea may also consider privately channeled welfare measures within corporate social responsibility mandates as given in the United Nation’s new norm for shared growth.
In this regard, Korea’s large conglomerates need to be proactive in nurturing human capital for a high-tech society.
Indeed, highly skilled manpower is the core element to ensuring a sustainable endogenous growth model.
Koreans should keep in mind that the provision of welfare should not discourage the incentive to work. We should not let our next generation bear the burden of financing excessive welfare expenditure for the present generation.
We should be aware of the fact that capitalism is not a static set of institutions but an evolutionary system that reinvents and reinvigorates itself through challenges and crisis. The present case of Greece’s demise makes this clear.<#sep#>-

MOD = 10**9 + 7

n, m = map(int, input().split())
p = input().strip()
L = len(p)

if m == 0:
    print(pow(26, n, MOD))
    exit()

y = list(map(int, input().split()))
y = [x-1 for x in y]  # convert to 0-based indices

for i in range(1, m):
    a = y[i-1]
    b = y[i]
    if b <= a + L - 1:
        offset = b - a
        req_len = a + L - b
        if p[offset:offset+req_len] != p[:req_len]:
            print(0)
            exit()

merged = []
for current_y in y:
    current_start = current_y
    current_end = current_y + L - 1
    if not merged:
        merged.append((current_start, current_end))
    else:
        last_start, last_end = merged[-1]
        if current_start <= last_end:
            new_start = last_start
            new_end = max(last_end, current_end)
            merged[-1] = (new_start, new_end)
        else:
            merged.append((current_start, current_end))

covered = sum(end - start + 1 for start, end in merged)
free = n - covered

print(pow(26, free, MOD))<#sep#>Analyze the interest rates of the notes issued by Microsoft Corporation as outlined in the Fourth Supplemental Indenture dated November 7, 2012. What does this indicate about the company's borrowing strategy?<#sep#>Let \( \triangle ABC \) be a right triangle with \( \angle A = 90^\circ \). Denote its incenter by \( I \), and let \( D \) be the intersection of \( BI \) with \( AC \) and \( E \) be the intersection of \( CI \) with \( AB \). Determine whether it is possible for the segments \( AB \), \( AC \), \( BI \), \( ID \), \( CI \), and \( IE \) to all have integer lengths.

<think>
Okay, let's tackle this problem step by step. So, we have a right triangle ABC with a right angle at A. The incenter is I. We need to check if it's possible for the lengths AB, AC, BI, ID, CI, and IE to all be integers. Hmm, interesting. Let me first recall some properties of right triangles and incenters.

First, in a right triangle, the inradius can be calculated using the formula r = (a + b - c)/2, where a and b are the legs and c is the hypotenuse. Since angle A is 90°, then BC is the hypotenuse. Let's denote AB = c, AC = b, and BC = a. Wait, no, actually, usually, in right triangles, the legs are a and b, and hypotenuse c. But let me confirm. Let's set AB and AC as the legs, and BC as the hypotenuse. Let's call AB = m, AC = n, so BC would be sqrt(m² + n²). The inradius r is (m + n - sqrt(m² + n²))/2. Since the inradius must be an integer if BI, ID, CI, IE are integers? Wait, but the problem doesn't mention the inradius being integer, but BI, ID, CI, IE. However, maybe the inradius is related to some of these segments. Let me think.

The incenter I is the intersection of the angle bisectors. So, BI and CI are angle bisectors from B and C, intersecting the inradius. Then, points D and E are where BI and CI intersect AC and AB, respectively. So, ID is part of BI from I to D on AC, and IE is part of CI from I to E on AB. All of these segments need to have integer lengths.

First, maybe assign coordinates to the triangle to make it easier. Let's place point A at (0,0), B at (0, m), and C at (n, 0). Then, BC is from (0, m) to (n, 0). The incenter I can be found using the formula for coordinates of the inradius. In a right triangle, the inradius is at (r, r), where r = (m + n - sqrt(m² + n²))/2. Wait, is that correct? Let's check.

The inradius in a right triangle is given by r = (a + b - c)/2, where a and b are the legs, c the hypotenuse. So here, legs AB = m, AC = n, so c = sqrt(m² + n²). Therefore, r = (m + n - sqrt(m² + n²))/2. Therefore, the inradius coordinates in the coordinate system where A is (0,0), B is (0, m), C is (n, 0) would be (r, r), because the inradius touches all sides, so the distance from A along both legs is r. Therefore, I is at (r, r).

Now, BI is the line from B (0, m) to I (r, r). Let's find the equation of BI. The slope would be (r - m)/(r - 0) = (r - m)/r. So the equation is y - m = [(r - m)/r](x - 0), so y = [(r - m)/r]x + m.

Similarly, CI is the line from C (n, 0) to I (r, r). The slope is (r - 0)/(r - n) = r/(r - n). The equation is y - 0 = [r/(r - n)](x - n), so y = [r/(r - n)](x - n).

Now, point D is the intersection of BI with AC. AC is the leg from A (0,0) to C (n, 0), so it's along the x-axis. Wait, no: AC is from A (0,0) to C (n,0), right? So AC is the horizontal leg. Wait, but in the coordinate system I set up, AB is vertical from (0,0) to (0, m), AC is horizontal to (n, 0), so BC is the hypotenuse. So, AC is the horizontal leg. Therefore, the line BI intersects AC at point D. Since AC is the x-axis, the intersection point D will have y-coordinate 0. Let's find where BI meets AC (y=0).

From BI's equation: y = [(r - m)/r]x + m. Set y=0:

0 = [(r - m)/r]x + m

=> [(r - m)/r]x = -m

=> x = (-m * r)/(r - m) = (m r)/(m - r)

Similarly, since r = (m + n - sqrt(m² + n²))/2, but this might complicate things. Let's note that m and n are integers, as AB and AC are supposed to be integers. So, we need m, n, BI, ID, CI, IE all integers.

m - t*(m - r) = 0

=> t = m / (m - r)

Therefore, the x-coordinate is t*r = (m / (m - r)) * r = (m r)/(m - r)

Therefore, point D is at ( (m r)/(m - r), 0 )

Similarly, ID is the distance from I (r, r) to D ( (m r)/(m - r), 0 )

So, coordinates of D: x = (m r)/(m - r), y = 0

So, ID's length is sqrt[ ( (m r/(m - r) - r )² + (0 - r)² ) ]

Compute x difference: ( (m r)/(m - r) - r ) = (m r - r(m - r))/(m - r) ) = (m r - m r + r²)/(m - r) ) = r² / (m - r)

Y difference: -r

Therefore, ID distance is sqrt[ (r² / (m - r))² + r² ]

= sqrt[ r^4 / (m - r)^2 + r^2 ]

= r * sqrt[ r² / (m - r)^2 + 1 ]

= r * sqrt[ (r² + (m - r)^2 ) / (m - r)^2 )

= r * sqrt( (r² + m² - 2 m r + r² ) / (m - r)^2 )

= r * sqrt( (2 r² - 2 m r + m² ) / (m - r)^2 )

= r * sqrt( (m² - 2 m r + 2 r² ) / (m - r)^2 )

Keep hearing aids away from heat and moisture.
Replace dead batteries immediately.
Clean hearing aids as instructed.
Do not use hairspray or other hair care products while wearing hearing aids.
Turn off hearing aids when they are not in use.
Keep replacement batteries and small aids away from children and pets.

Certain illnesses, such as heart disease, high blood pressure, and diabetes, put ears at risk by interfering with the ears' blood supply. Otosclerosis is a bone disease of the middle ear and Ménière's disease affects the inner ear. Both can cause hearing loss.

Maybe there's a better way to approach this. Let's think of m and n as integers, and r = (m + n - sqrt(m² + n²))/2. Wait, but m and n are the legs, so BC = sqrt(m² + n²). Let's denote BC = h, so h = sqrt(m² + n²). Then r = (m + n - h)/2. Therefore, h = m + n - 2 r. Since h must be a positive number, m + n - 2 r > 0, so r < (m + n)/2, which makes sense because inradius is smaller.

But m, n, h form a Pythagorean triple since ABC is a right triangle with integer sides. Wait, but the problem states AB, AC are integers, but BC can be irrational unless it's a Pythagorean triple. However, the problem says "the segments AB, AC, BI, ID, CI, and IE to all have integer lengths". So AB and AC are legs, so integers, but BC is hypotenuse, which may not be integer. However, BI, ID, CI, IE need to be integers. Wait, but if BC is not integer, then h is sqrt(m² + n²), which could be irrational, but the other segments are BI, ID, etc. Maybe h doesn't need to be integer, but BI, ID, CI, IE have to be.

But how can BI and CI be integers if h is not? Let's think. Since BI is the distance from B to I. Coordinates of B are (0, m), I is (r, r). So BI is sqrt( (r - 0)^2 + (r - m)^2 ) = sqrt(r² + (m - r)^2 ). Similarly, CI is sqrt( (n - r)^2 + (0 - r)^2 ) = sqrt( (n - r)^2 + r² )

Therefore, BI and CI must be integers. Also, ID and IE must be integers. So, we have four distances that need to be integers: BI, ID, CI, IE, in addition to AB and AC (m and n) being integers.

So, perhaps it's necessary that m, n, BI, ID, CI, IE are integers. Let's note that r is (m + n - h)/2, where h = sqrt(m² + n²). So r is a rational number if m and n are integers, but h is irrational unless it's a Pythagorean triple. But r being rational may not be necessary, but BI and CI need to be integers. However, if m and n are integers, then BI and CI are sqrt(r² + (m - r)^2 ) and sqrt( (n - r)^2 + r² ), which may not necessarily be integers even if r is rational. But maybe m, n can be chosen such that these expressions result in integers. This seems tricky. Let's try to look for specific examples.

Let me consider a Pythagorean triple where m and n are integers and h is integer. For example, the 3-4-5 triangle. Let's take AB = 3, AC = 4, so BC = 5. Then, the inradius r = (3 + 4 - 5)/2 = 2/2 = 1. So r = 1. Then, coordinates of I are (1,1). Then BI is the distance from B (0,3) to I (1,1): sqrt(1² + (-2)^2) = sqrt(1 + 4) = sqrt(5), which is irrational. So BI is not integer here. Similarly, CI is distance from C (4,0) to I (1,1): sqrt( (4 -1)^2 + (0 -1)^2 ) = sqrt(9 + 1) = sqrt(10), which is also irrational. So in this case, BI and CI are irrational. So that doesn't work. Let's check another triple.

How about 5-12-13. Then r = (5 + 12 - 13)/2 = 4/2 = 2. So I is at (2,2). BI is distance from (0,12) to (2,2): sqrt(2² + (-10)^2) = sqrt(4 + 100) = sqrt(104) = 2*sqrt(26), still irrational. CI is distance from (5,0) to (2,2): sqrt(3² + (-2)^2) = sqrt(9 + 4) = sqrt(13), irrational. Hmm.

So maybe if we take a different triple where r is such that BI and CI are integers. Let's suppose m and n are such that r is such that sqrt(r² + (m - r)^2 ) and sqrt( (n - r)^2 + r² ) are integers.

Alternatively, perhaps m and n need to be chosen so that the expressions under the square roots become perfect squares.

Let me denote BI² = r² + (m - r)^2 = 2 r² - 2 m r + m².

Similarly, CI² = (n - r)^2 + r² = 2 r² - 2 n r + n².

So both BI² and CI² must be perfect squares.

Given that r = (m + n - h)/2, where h = sqrt(m² + n²). Hmm, this seems complicated because h is sqrt(m² + n²). Let me see if there is a way to express r in terms of m and n. Since h = sqrt(m² + n²), so:

r = (m + n - sqrt(m² + n²))/2.

Therefore, BI² = 2 [ (m + n - h)/2 ]² - 2 m [ (m + n - h)/2 ] + m².

Let me compute that step by step.

First, expand BI²:

= 2*( (m + n - h)/2 )² - 2m*( (m + n - h)/2 ) + m²

Let me compute each term:

First term: 2*( (m + n - h)^2 / 4 ) = (m + n - h)^2 / 2

Second term: -2m*( (m + n - h)/2 ) = -m*(m + n - h )

Third term: + m²

So combining:

BI² = (m + n - h)^2 / 2 - m(m + n - h) + m²

Let me factor out (m + n - h):

= (m + n - h)[ (m + n - h)/2 - m ] + m²

Compute the term inside the brackets:

( (m + n - h)/2 - m ) = (m + n - h - 2m)/2 = ( -m + n - h )/2

Thus,

BI² = (m + n - h)( -m + n - h )/2 + m²

Multiply out the numerator:

(m + n - h)(-m + n - h) = [ (n - h) + m ][ (n - h) - m ] = (n - h)^2 - m^2

Therefore,

BI² = [ (n - h)^2 - m² ] / 2 + m² = [ (n - h)^2 - m² + 2m² ] / 2 = [ (n - h)^2 + m² ] / 2

But h = sqrt(m² + n²). Let me substitute:

(n - sqrt(m² + n²))^2 + m² all over 2.

Expand (n - h)^2:

= n² - 2 n h + h²

So, substituting:

BI² = (n² - 2 n h + h² + m² ) / 2

But h² = m² + n², so substitute that:

= (n² - 2 n h + m² + n² + m² ) / 2

Combine terms:

= (2 m² + 2 n² - 2 n h ) / 2

= m² + n² - n h

But h = sqrt(m² + n²), so substitute again:

BI² = m² + n² - n sqrt(m² + n²)

Similarly, this is equal to:

= h² - n h

= h (h - n )

But h = sqrt(m² + n²), which is sqrt(n² + m²). Therefore, BI² = h (h - n )

Hmm, but since h = sqrt(m² + n²), h - n = sqrt(m² + n²) - n. Let me rationalize this:

sqrt(m² + n²) - n = m² / (sqrt(m² + n²) + n )

<#sep#>1:{F(1)}}

 spaces can be eliminated:

memo={1:{F(1)}}

This is 14.

Thus total first line length: 30 +1 +14 = 45 characters.

The second line is the function definition and the rest:

def f(n,m=memo): if n in m:return m[n] s=set() for k in range(1,n): a=f(k);b=f(n-k) s.update(x+y for x in a for y in b); s.update(x*y/(x+y) for x in a for y in b) m[n]=s return m[n]

+def _validate_lock_params_match_lock(
+        lock_client, name, resource_group_name, resource_provider_namespace,
+        parent_resource_path, resource_type, resource_name):
+    '''
+    Locks are scoped to subscription, resource group or resource.
+    However, the az list command returns all locks for the current scopes
+    and all lower scopes (e.g. resource group level also includes resource locks).
+    This can lead to a confusing user experience where the user specifies a lock
+    name and assumes that it will work, even if they haven't given the right
+    scope. This function attempts to validate the parameters and help the
+    user find the right scope, by first finding the lock, and then infering
+    what it's parameters should be.
+    '''
+    locks = lock_client.management_locks.list_at_subscription_level()
+    found_count = 0 # locks at different levels can have the same name
+    lock_resource_id = None
+    for lock in locks:
+        if lock.name == name:
+            found_count = found_count + 1
+            lock_resource_id = lock.id
+    if found_count == 1:
+        # If we only found one lock, let's validate that the parameters are correct,
+        # if we found more than one, we'll assume the user knows what they're doing
+        # TODO: Add validation for that case too?
+        resource = parse_resource_id(lock_resource_id)
+        _resource_group = resource.get('resource_group', None)
+        _resource_namespace = resource.get('namespace', None)
+        if _resource_group is None:
+            return
+        if resource_group_name != _resource_group:
+            raise CLIError(
+                'Unexpected --resource-group for lock {}, expected {}'.format(
+                    name, _resource_group))
+        if _resource_namespace is None:
+            return
+        if resource_provider_namespace != _resource_namespace:
+            raise CLIError(
+                'Unexpected --namespace for lock {}, expected {}'.format(name, _resource_namespace))
+        if resource.get('grandchild_type', None) is None:
+            _resource_type = resource.get('type', None)
+            _resource_name = resource.get('name', None)
+        else:
+            _resource_type = resource.get('child_type', None)
+            _resource_name = resource.get('child_name', None)
+            parent = (resource['type'] + '/' +  resource['name'])
+            if parent != parent_resource_path:
+                raise CLIError(
+                    'Unexpected --parent for lock {}, expected {}'.format(
+                        name, parent))
+        if resource_type != _resource_type:
+            raise CLIError('Unexpected --resource-type for lock {}, expected {}'.format(
+                name, _resource_type))
+        if resource_name != _resource_name:
+            raise CLIError('Unexpected --resource-name for lock {}, expected {}'.format(
+                name, _resource_name))
+

Alternatively, maybe there exist some non-primitive triples where BI and CI are integers. Let's see. Let's consider scaling up a triangle where r is an integer. Then BI² = sqrt(r² + (m - r)^2 ) = sqrt(k² + (3k -k)^2 ) = sqrt(k² + (2k)^2 )=sqrt(k² +4k²)=sqrt(5k²)=k*sqrt(5). So unless k is a multiple of sqrt(5), which would not be integer, BI is irrational. So scaling a primitive triple does not help here.

Alternatively, maybe there exists a specific Pythagorean triple where BI and CI are integers. Let's try to find such a triple.

Suppose we have a Pythagorean triple (m, n, h). Then, BI² = m² + n² - n h = h² - n h = h (h - n ). Similarly, CI² = h (h - m ). So BI² = h (h - n ), CI² = h (h - m ). Therefore, for BI² and CI² to be perfect squares, h (h - n ) and h (h - m ) must be perfect squares.

Given that h, m, n form a Pythagorean triple, with m² + n² = h². Therefore, h - n and h - m are positive integers. Let me denote d = h - n, e = h - m. Then, h = n + d = m + e. Therefore, m² + n² = (n + d )². Expanding:

m² = (n + d )² - n² = 2 n d + d²

Similarly, m = sqrt(2 n d + d² )

But m must be integer, so 2 n d + d² must be a perfect square. Similarly, since h = m + e, substituting into Pythagorean equation:

m² + n² = (m + e )² = m² + 2 m e + e²

e² + d² -50(e + d ) +625=0

from fractions import Fraction as F
memo={1:{F(1)}}
def g(n,m=memo):
 if n in m:return m[n]
 s=set()
 for k in range(1,n):
  a=g(k);b=g(n-k)
  s|={x+y for x in a for y in b}
  s|={x*y/(x+y)for x in a for y in b}
 m[n]=s
 return m[n]

- Amadou Ballaké et Les 5 Consuls - Ligda remba. 45 rpm disc : Amadou Ballaké et Les 5 Consuls - A la mémoire du regretté Demba / Ligda remba. Amadou Balaké was born in 1944 in Ouahigouya, in the northern-east of Burkina Faso. After the death of his father, he relocated with his mother in the capital of the country, Ouagadougou, where he began to meet several local musicians. Shortly after he went to Mali to work as a chauffeur apprentice, and only returned to Ouagadougou six years later. He worked some time in construction, and then became a taximan. After the loss of his vehicle, he went to Mali and was engaged as a professional musician in the Grand Hotel orchestra. In 1963 he left Mali to play in the Tropicana orchestra in Abidjan, capital of the Ivory Coast, but only six months later he went to Guinea. Leader of the Bafinf Jazz band, he participated there in many musical competitions, and regularly played for president Sékou Touré meetings across the country. He returned to Burkina at the end of the 60's, and became very popular in the country thanks to his work with the Harmonie Voltaïque band. Convided by the Nigerian producer Aboudou Lassissi, he went back to Abidjan at the beginning of the 70's .

import bisect

n = int(input())
entry_rays = list(map(int, input().split()))
exit_rays = list(map(int, input().split()))

exit_pos = {ray: idx for idx, ray in enumerate(exit_rays)}
sequence = [exit_pos[r] for r in entry_rays]

# Compute LDS by finding LIS of negated sequence
neg_seq = [-x for x in sequence]
tails = []

for num in neg_seq:
    idx = bisect.bisect_left(tails, num)
    if idx == len(tails):
        tails.append(num)
    else:
        tails[idx] = num

print(len(tails))<#sep#>Compare the tracking errors of the Vanguard ESG USD Corporate Bond UCITS ETF (EUR-Hedged) and the Vanguard ESG USD Corporate Bond UCITS ETF (GBP-Hedged). Kandy is afraid to code right now. So, he mails you the required conditions to calculate the number of possible Good Strings. Some properties of these strings include:-
They are of given specific length. 
They consist of digits only between 0-9 (both inclusive). 
They may have leading zeros. 
They have certain Good Product which is defined as product of
   digits in the string.
Their F(Good Product) is always greater than 0.

F(P) is calculated as Bitwise XOR of all the prime factors of P.

For eg: 12 is written as 22 * 31
Therefore, F(12) = 2 xor 2 xor 3 = 3

Input:
First line of the input consists of number of testcases T
Next T lines consists of length of the Good String N

Output:
Print the number of possible Good Strings modulo 10^9+7 of given length N for each test case in a separate line.

Constraints:
Subtask 1:
1 ≤ T ≤ 100
1 ≤ N ≤ 105
Subtask 2:
1 ≤ T ≤ 10
1 ≤ N ≤ 1018

Note:
Leading zeros in the string also contribute to its product of digits which would be zero anyway.

SAMPLE INPUT
5
1
5
7
8
2

SAMPLE OUTPUT
6
52806
4370778
39585232
64

Explanation

[Case 1:] For N = 1, possible evil strings are "2","3","5","6","7","8". Their F(Evil Product) is greater than 0.

Rest of the cases is for you to figure out.

gpt: <think> Okay, I need to solve this problem where I have to find the number of "Good Strings" of a given length N. Let me try to understand the problem step by step.

First, the properties of a Good String:

1. The string is of a given length N, consisting of digits 0-9. It can have leading zeros.
2. The Good Product is the product of all digits in the string.
3. The F(Good Product) must be greater than 0.
4. F(P) is the XOR of all prime factors of P. Wait, how exactly is F(P) calculated? For example, in the sample for 12, the prime factors are 2² × 3¹. The factors listed are 2, 2, 3, so XOR gives 3. Hmm, right.

The task is to count how many such strings exist for each N, modulo 1e9+7.

Hmm. The key point here is that F(P) must be > 0. So I need to find all numbers P which equal the product of the digits of the string, such that when we take all their prime factors, their XOR is positive.

Wait, but the product of the digits could be zero? Oh, but the problem says "They have certain Good Product which is defined as product of digits in the string." But for the product to be zero, at least one digit has to be zero. Wait, but in that case, any such string with a zero would have a Good Product of zero. Then F(0) is undefined? Or maybe zero?

First, what is F(P) when P is zero?

So the product of digits could be a square of a prime, so in that case, F(P) would be zero, which would make it invalid. 

Ah! So the problem isn't just about not having zeros, but the F(P) must be greater than zero. 

So the criteria for a string to be good are:

- All digits are from 1-9 (since zeros can't be there as their products would let F be zero)
AND
- The product of the digits must have the property that when taking all prime factors (with multiplicity) of the product, their XOR is greater than zero.

F(6) is 2 and 3 → 2^3= 1 (binary 02 vs 10) their XOR is 2 XOR3 = 1.

So F(6)=1>0. 

Hmm so, the problem is for all P ≥1 (because in the allowed case, all digits are 1-9, so product can be 1 or more) we need F(P) >0.

**Install Method:** How did you install the CLI? (e.g. pip, interactive script, apt-get, Docker, MSI, nightly)

**CLI Version:** What version of the CLI and modules are installed? (Use `az --version`)  
Answer here: azure-cli (2.0.3)

**OS Version:** What OS and version are you using?  
Answer here: macOS 10.12.3

**Shell Type:** What shell are you using? (e.g. bash, cmd.exe, Bash on Windows)  
Answer here: bash on macOS

---

### Description

It would be great if when we create a storage account (or other resources) that if we specify a resource group, we can default to use its location. Otherwise we do this ...
```
az group create -n PapaResourceGroup -l eastus

az storage account create -n papastorage -g PapaResourceGroup --sku Standard_LRS -l eastus
```

Notice we type the location twice. If I omit the location in the second command, it fails. We also should be able to default the sku. A quicker way would look like this ...

```
az group create -n PapaResourceGroup -l eastus

az storage account create -n papastorage -g PapaResourceGroup

d, k, a, b, t = map(int, input().split())

if d <= k:
    print(d * a)
else:
    time1 = k * a + (d - k) * b
    m_max = d // k
    s = d - m_max * k
    # Calculate time2
    option_drive = t + s * a
    option_walk = s * b
    time2 = m_max * k * a + (m_max - 1) * t + min(option_drive, option_walk)
    
    candidates = [time1, time2]
    
    if m_max > 1:
        m_prev = m_max - 1
        s_prev = d - m_prev * k
        q_prev = s_prev // k
        r_prev = s_prev % k
        # Calculate time to drive s_prev km after repair
        drive_time = t + q_prev * (t + k * a) + r_prev * a
        # Compare with walking s_prev km
        option_prev = min(drive_time, s_prev * b)
        time3 = m_prev * k * a + (m_prev - 1) * t + option_prev
        candidates.append(time3)
    
    print(min(candidates))<#sep#>**Financial Ratios Calculation**: If you were to calculate the Price-to-Earnings (P/E) ratio for Westgold Resources Ltd. and Myer Holdings Ltd., what additional financial data would you need, and why is this ratio important for investors?<#sep#>. Similarly, the intersection point D is on AC, which is the x-axis. We found D's coordinates as ( (m r)/(m - r), 0 ). Similarly, E is on AB, which is the y-axis. Let's find E's coordinates.

The line CI goes from C (n, 0) to I (r, r). The equation of CI is y = [r/(r - n)] (x - n ). To find E, which is the intersection with AB. AB is the line x=0. So substitute x=0 into CI's equation: y = [r/(r - n ) ] ( -n ) = - (r n )/(r - n ) = (r n )/(n - r ). Therefore, E is at (0, (r n )/(n - r )).

Therefore, IE is the distance from I (r, r) to E (0, (r n )/(n - r )).

Coordinates of E: (0, (r n )/(n - r ))

Therefore, IE distance is sqrt[ (r -0 )² + ( r - (r n )/(n - r ) )² ]

= sqrt[ r² + ( r - (r n )/(n - r ) )² ]

Simplify the y-component:

r - (r n )/(n - r ) = r [1 - n/(n - r ) ] = r [ (n - r - n ) / (n - r ) ] = r [ (- r ) / (n - r ) ] = - r² / (n - r )

Therefore, IE distance is sqrt[ r² + ( - r² / (n - r ) )² ] = sqrt[ r² + r^4 / (n - r )² ] = r sqrt[ 1 + r² / (n - r )² ] = r sqrt[ ( (n - r )² + r² ) / (n - r )² ] = sqrt[ ( (n - r )² + r² ) / ( (n - r )² ) ] * r = sqrt( (n² - 2 n r + 2 r² ) ) * r / |n - r |

But this is similar to the expression we had for ID.

Alternatively, note that in a right triangle, the inradius is r = (m + n - h)/2, and h = sqrt(m² + n²). So, if m, n are integers, h is rational only if (m, n, h) is a Pythagorean triple, in which case h is integer. Otherwise, h is irrational. So, if h is irrational, then r = (m + n - h)/2 is irrational as well. Then BI and CI are distances involving r, which would also be irrational. Therefore, to have BI and CI integers, h must be integer, so m, n, h must form a Pythagorean triple, and r must be rational (specifically, r = (m + n - h)/2 must be a rational number). Since m, n, h are integers, r is either integer or half-integer.

But even when r is integer, as in the 3-4-5 triangle, BI and CI are irrational. Therefore, it seems challenging to have BI and CI as integers.

However, maybe there exist some Pythagorean triples where both h*(h -n ) and h*(h -m ) are perfect squares. Let's see. Suppose m=15, n=20, h=25. Then, check h*(h -n )=25*5=125, which is not a square. Similarly, h*(h -m )=25*10=250, not a square.

Another triple: 7-24-25. Then, h=25, h -m=18, h*(h -m)=25*18=450, not a square. h -n=1, h*(h -n)=25*1=25, which is square. So BI=5, but m=7, n=24. Then, compute CI: distance from C (24,0) to I (r, r ). r=(7+24 -25)/2=(6)/2=3. So I=(3,3). CI is sqrt( (24-3)^2 + (0 -3)^2 )=sqrt(21² +3²)=sqrt(441 +9)=sqrt(450)=15*sqrt(2), not integer.

Wait, but r=3 in this case, which is integer. BI=5, which is integer. But ID and IE?

Let's compute ID. Point D is on AC at ( (m r )/(m - r ), 0 ) with m=7, r=3. So D_x=(7*3)/(7 -3 )=21/4=5.25. So coordinates of D=(21/4,0). Then ID is distance from (3,3) to (21/4,0). So difference in x: 21/4 -3= 21/4 -12/4=9/4. Difference in y:0 -3=-3. So ID= sqrt( (9/4)^2 + (-3)^2 )=sqrt(81/16 +9 )=sqrt(81/16 +144/16 )=sqrt(225/16 )=15/4=3.75, which is not integer. Similarly, IE: point E is on AB at (0, (r n )/(n - r ))=(0, (3*24 )/(24 -3 ))=(0,72/21)=(0,24/7). So coordinates of E=(0,24/7). Then IE is distance from (3,3) to (0,24/7). Difference in x:3-0=3. Difference in y:3 -24/7=21/7 -24/7= -3/7. So IE= sqrt(3² + (-3/7 )² )=sqrt(9 +9/49 )=sqrt(441/49 +9/49 )=sqrt(450/49 )=(sqrt(450))/7=(15 sqrt(2))/7, which is irrational. So in this case, BI is integer, but ID, IE, CI are not.

Hence, even in cases where BI is integer, other segments are not. It's challenging to find a case where all BI, ID, CI, IE are integers. Let me try to formalize the conditions.

We need:

1. AB = m, AC = n integers.

2. BI, ID, CI, IE integers.

Since ABC is a right triangle with legs m, n and hypotenuse h.

Incenter I has coordinates (r, r), with r = (m + n - h)/2.

BI is sqrt(r² + (m - r )² )

ID is sqrt( (mr/(m - r ) - r )² + r² )

Similarly, CI is sqrt( (n - r )² + r² )

IE is sqrt( r² + ( r - rn/(n - r ) )² )

These all need to be integers.

Let me consider ID:

ID = sqrt( ( (mr/(m - r ) - r )² + r² )

Simplify the x-component:

mr/(m - r ) - r = (mr - r(m - r )) / (m - r ) = (mr - mr + r² )/(m - r )= r²/(m - r )

Therefore, ID = sqrt( (r²/(m - r ))² + r² ) = r * sqrt( r²/(m - r )² +1 ) = r * sqrt( (r² + (m - r )² )/(m - r )² ) = sqrt( r² + (m - r )² ) * r / (m - r )

But sqrt(r² + (m - r )² ) is BI, which must be integer. So ID = BI * r / (m - r )

Similarly, since ID must be integer, then BI * r must be divisible by (m - r ). But BI and r are related through the Pythagorean theorem.

Similarly, for IE, we can derive IE = CI * r / (n - r )

1. BI = sqrt(r² + (m - r )² ) is integer.

n, c = map(int, input().split())
p = list(map(int, input().split()))
s = list(map(int, input().split()))

total_excess = 0
total_deficit = 0
def_j = [0] * n

for j in range(n):
    if s[j] > p[j]:
        def_j[j] = 1
        total_deficit += s[j] - p[j]
    elif p[j] > s[j]:
        total_excess += p[j] - s[j]

# Compute suffix counts of def_j
suffix_def = [0] * (n + 1)
for i in range(n-1, -1, -1):
    suffix_def[i] = suffix_def[i+1] + def_j[i]

K = 0
for i in range(n):
    if p[i] > s[i]:
        K += suffix_def[i+1]

transfer = min(total_excess, total_deficit, c * K)
initial_sum = sum(min(p_i, s_i) for p_i, s_i in zip(p, s))

print(initial_sum + transfer)<#sep#>**Investment Diversification Strategy**: Given the variety of companies listed, how would you construct a diversified investment portfolio using these companies? Wait, we have to take primes where their bit is 1 (odd exponent), and XOR their prime values. 

The XOR is an operation between numbers, so 2 and 3 would give 2^3 =1, but for example 2 XOR 3 XOR 5 would be their binary XORs.

So the key is the final vector of the sum mod2 of all digits' contributions. The XOR of the primes set in the vector must be not zero.

Therefore, for the product to be good, the XOR of the primes set in the final vector must not be zero. 

So how can I model this?

The approach is to model this with dynamic programming, where the state is the current vector (there are 4 bits → 16 possible states). 

The idea is that for each position in the string (digit in the sequence), we track all possible total parity vectors (the 16 states) and count the number of ways to achieve them with the digits chosen so far. 

Then, after N digits, we can look at all states where the corresponding XOR of the set primes (those with 1s in their vector) is non-zero, and sum those counts. 

However, this approach would be feasible for N up to, say, 1e5, but the constraints mention N up to 1e18, which suggests that linear DP is impossible, and we need a matrix exponentiation approach.

Transition between the vectors can be represented as a matrix exponentiation problem.

First, let's confirm the possible variables for the DP. Let's consider the current state S (vector of 4 bits). For each new digit, the next state S' is the XOR of S and the vector of the digit's contribution (since each current prime's parity is added by the contribution mod 2 → so mod2 addition = XOR).

Because addition mod2 is equivalent to XOR. 

So for each digit's contribution vector D, when moving from state S to a new state S' = S XOR D.

Therefore, transitions between the state S are multiplicative with each step, so we can represent transitions via a transition matrix (a 16x16 matrix, since there are 16 possible states). 

We can model this as a vector: for each digit, the number of times each possible vector can transition into others when multiplied by choosing a digit. 

The transitions are additive. So, building a transition matrix where the entry m[i][j] is the number of possible digits (from 1-9) that would transition from state i to state j. 

Wait, wait. Let me think of it this way:

Suppose each state is a 4-bit number (from 0 to 15). 

Each digit contributes a vector D. 

The transition from state 'current' is to current XOR D, for each possible D from the 9 digits. 

Suppose we have a transition count matrix T where T[a][b] is the number of digits d in 1-9 where D (the vector of d) has the following: 

current XOR D becomes b → so the digit contributes a value such that D = (b XOR current).

Therefore for each current state, the next state is S XOR D → So transitions are such that D must be (next_state) XOR current_state. 

Wait, for a given current state and next state, how many digits have D such that (current's vector) XOR D = next state → D must be current XOR next_state? 

Wait, yes. So for each transition from current to next, the number of digits that can do that is the number of digits whose own vector D equals (current XOR next). 

Thus, the transition matrix T can be computed as for each entry a, b (current, next), T[a][b] = count of digits where the digit's vector is equal to (a ^ b). 

Therefore, the transition matrix can be built as follows:

Precompute for all possible a and b, the number of digits with vector (a ^ b). 

First, let me precompute for all digit vectors:

Let me list all digits and their vectors:

+# --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
+# Copyright (c) Microsoft Corporation. All rights reserved.
+# Licensed under the MIT License. See License.txt in the project root for license information.
+# --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
+
+import uuid
+from azure.cli.core.util import CLIError
+from ._client_factory import _graph_client_factory
+
+VARIANT_GROUP_ID_ARGS = ['object_id', 'group_id', 'group_object_id']
+
+
+def validate_group(namespace):
+    # For AD auto-commands, here we resolve logic names to object ids needed by SDK methods
+    attr, value = next(((x, getattr(namespace, x)) for x in VARIANT_GROUP_ID_ARGS
+                        if hasattr(namespace, x)))
+    try:
+        uuid.UUID(value)
+    except ValueError:
+        client = _graph_client_factory()
+        sub_filters = []
+        sub_filters.append("startswith(displayName,'{}')".format(value))
+        sub_filters.append("displayName eq '{}'".format(value))
+        result = list(client.groups.list(filter=' or '.join(sub_filters)))
+        count = len(result)
+        if count == 1:
+            setattr(namespace, attr, result[0].object_id)
+        elif count == 0:
+            raise CLIError("No group matches the name of '{}'".format(value))
+        else:
+            raise CLIError("More than one groups match the name of '{}'".format(value))
+
+
+def validate_member_id(namespace):
+    from azure.cli.core._profile import Profile, CLOUD
+    try:
+        uuid.UUID(namespace.url)
+        profile = Profile()
+        _, _, tenant_id = profile.get_login_credentials()
+        graph_url = CLOUD.endpoints.active_directory_graph_resource_id
+        namespace.url = '{}{}/directoryObjects/{}'.format(graph_url, tenant_id,
+                                                          namespace.url)
+    except ValueError:
+        pass  # let it go, invalid values will be caught by server anyway

+cli_command(__name__, 'ad group create',<#sep#>An EV’s speed controller is the equivalent of the carburetor or fuel-injection system in an ICE vehicle. To control the vehicle’s speed, the controller takes the energy from the battery pack and feeds it to the motor in a regulated manner. Modern controllers do this by pulse-width modulation, taking the full voltage from the battery pack and feeding it to the motor in thousands of tiny on–off pulses per second. The longer the duration, or “width” of the “on” pulses, the more electricity the motor receives and the faster the vehicle moves. Because the pulses are so tiny, the process feels completely smooth to the driver.
EVs can have AC motors or DC motors, and each needs its own kind of controller. In EVs with AC motors, an AC controller must first convert the DC from the batteries into AC before feeding it to the motor.
How does the controller know how much energy to give the motor? The potbox tells it. This linear potentiometer is a sensor that produces a resistance output proportional to its displacement or position. It responds to the driver’s foot pressure on the throttle pedal and sends a corresponding signal to the controller. The throttle pedal in an EV works just as it does in an ICE vehicle—the more you depress it, the faster you go.
The motor is the brawn of the EV, converting electrical energy from the batteries into mechanical energy to propel the vehicle. Instead of invisible electrons flowing through wires, we now have rotating components.
It’s the relationship between electricity and magnetism that enables the motor to do work. Passing electricity through a wire creates a magnetic field around the wire. By winding wire in a motor and running electricity through it, magnetic poles that repel each other are created, causing the shaft of the motor to spin.
If the EV has regenerative braking, the motor can also act as a generator. When the vehicle is coasting or braking, the momentum of the car drives the motor—rather than the motor driving the car. The magnetic fields induce current in the wires, the flip side of the process described above. The electricity flows backward through the controller (which rectifies it from AC back into DC) and into the battery pack. This process also creates drag on the motor—the “braking” part of regenerative braking, which is very similar to what happens in an ICE car when you lift your foot off the throttle in a low gear. Though it’s an intrinsic part of AC drive systems, regenerative braking is more rare in DC systems, where a special controller and extra wiring are required to allow the motor to serve as a generator.
The energy output from the spinning shaft of the motor now needs to reach the drive wheels. On a very small EV, the motor might drive the wheels directly, but with full-size vehicles, at least one level of gear reduction is necessary to reduce the revolutions per minute (rpm) of the motor to a usable speed at the wheels. A “direct-drive” vehicle will have a single gear reduction, which might be a gearbox or a belt-and-pulley arrangement. No shifting is necessary. This is common with AC motors that have upper limits of 8,000 to 13,000 rpm. DC motors with limits of about 5,000 to 6,000 rpm usually use the same kind of multiple-gear manual transmissions found in ICE cars. In EVs with manual transmissions, the clutch is usually retained and works the same as in an ICE vehicle.<#sep#>-

n, m = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

original_set = set()
seen = set()
duplicates_indices = []

for idx, num in enumerate(a):
    if num in seen:
        duplicates_indices.append(idx)
    else:
        seen.add(num)
        original_set.add(num)

original_unique_even = sum(1 for x in original_set if x % 2 == 0)
original_unique_odd = len(original_set) - original_unique_even
k = len(duplicates_indices)
required_e = max(0, (n // 2) - original_unique_even)
required_o = max(0, (n // 2) - original_unique_odd)

if required_e + required_o != k:
    print(-1)
    exit()

available_even = []
current = 2
while current <= m and len(available_even) < required_e:
    if current not in original_set:
        available_even.append(current)
    current += 2

available_odd = []
current = 1
while current <= m and len(available_odd) < required_o:
    if current not in original_set:
        available_odd.append(current)
    current += 2

if len(available_even) < required_e or len(available_odd) < required_o:
    print(-1)
    exit()

for i in range(len(duplicates_indices)):
    idx = duplicates_indices[i]
    if i < required_e:
        a[idx] = available_even[i]
    else:
        a[idx] = available_odd[i - required_e]

current_even = original_unique_even + len(available_even)
current_odd = original_unique_odd + len(available_odd)

if current_even != n // 2 or current_odd != n // 2:
    print(-1)
    exit()

print(k)

Let me set h*(h -n )=a² and h*(h -m )=b². Then:

h -n = a² /h

h -m =b² /h

Thus, n = h -a² /h

m= h -b² /h

Substituting into m² +n² =h²:

(h -b²/h )² + (h -a²/h )² =h²

Expand:

h² - 2 b² + b⁴/h² + h² - 2 a² +a⁴/h² = h²

Combine terms:

2 h² - 2 (a² + b² ) + (a⁴ + b⁴)/h² =h²

Bring all terms to one side:

2 h² - 2 (a² + b² ) + (a⁴ + b⁴)/h² -h²=0

h² -2(a² +b² ) + (a⁴ +b⁴)/h²=0

Multiply through by h²:

h⁴ -2(a² +b² ) h² + (a⁴ +b⁴ )=0

This is a quadratic in h²:

Let x=h². Then:

x² -2(a² +b² )x + (a⁴ +b⁴ )=0

Solve for x:

x = [2(a² +b² ) ± sqrt(4(a² +b² )² -4(a⁴ +b⁴ )) ]/2

Simplify discriminant:

4(a² +b² )² -4(a⁴ +b⁴ )=4[ (a^4 +2 a² b² +b^4 ) -a^4 -b^4 ]=4[2 a² b² ]=8 a² b²

Therefore:

x= [2(a² +b² ) ± sqrt(8 a² b² ) ]/2 = [2(a² +b² ) ± 2 a b sqrt(2 ) ]/2= (a² +b² ) ± a b sqrt(2 )

But x=h² must be positive real number. However, unless sqrt(2 ) is rational, which it's not, x would not be a perfect square unless the term with sqrt(2 ) cancels out. This would require that (a² +b² ) and a b sqrt(2 ) are both integers. For that, a b must be a multiple of sqrt(2 ), but a and b are integers, so this is impossible. Therefore, there are no integer solutions for h, a, b in this case.

This suggests that there are no Pythagorean triples where both h*(h -n ) and h*(h -m ) are perfect squares, meaning that BI and CI cannot both be integers in a right triangle with integer sides.

Thus, let me make a dictionary:

count_for_vector():

0 →3,1 →1, 2→1, 4→1, 8→2, 12 →1.

Others (3,5,6,7,9, etc.) →0. 

So this is the distribution. 

Alright, so transitions:

The transition matrix entries T[a][b] is equal to the count of digits d such that the vector of d is (a XOR b).

Because the transition from a → b requires that d's vector is (a ^ b), so that a XOR (d_vec) = b → rearranged: d_vec = a XOR b.

Thus for the matrix entry between a → b is the count of digits with vector (a XOR (a XOR b))? or wait more clearly: the digits that have vector D = (new_state XOR current_state). 

Therefore, the number of such digits for each a and B is the count_for_vector[a XOR B].

So to compute T, for each possible a (current state), b (next state), T[a][b] = count_for_vector( a ^ b). 

So constructing the transition matrix is manageable because each entry is the count for a vector determined by a^b.

Now, the problem is that the DP transitions can be modeled as a transition matrix multiplication. 

The state is the current vector. After each step, the count of each vector is equal to the sum over (previous state) multiplied by the transition. 

The initial state before any digits are considered is 0 (since no primes have been counted). Wait, because initially, the product is 1 (empty product), so the exponents for all primes are zero. Thus the initial vector is 0.

Wait, when the product is 1 (all digits are 1?), then the product's primes are all even exponents (0), so vector 0. 

Wait for the first digit: choosing a digit, say digit2, adds its factors 2^1, so the new vector would be 8 (for the current state was 0, then XOR with 8 gives 8, so the state transitions to 8. 

The DP array starts as a vector where the initial state (vector0) has count1.

Each step, you multiply the DP vector by the transition matrix. 

So the total after N steps (digits) will be the application of the transition matrix to the DP vector N times. 

Once we can represent the transitions as a matrix, we can compute the result efficiently using matrix exponentiation, even for N up to 1e18. 

The steps are:

1. Precompute the transition matrix T that transitions from any current state a to next state b with T[a][b] being the number of digits d where their vector D is a^b. 

2. The matrix is of size 16x16 since there are 16 possible states (vectors of 4 bits).

3. The initial vector is a vector with a single entry (state 0) of count 1. But each step's vector is transformed via the transition matrix. 

Wait actually, the initial count is for how many ways with zero digits chosen (but since N is given, perhaps the initial vector is for one element after zero digits). 

Wait, perhaps better to model the state as a vector of size 16 (number of states) where each entry s[v] is the number of ways to have vector v after k steps. So after N steps, the total number of good strings would be the sum over all v where the XOR of the primes set in v is not zero, of the s[v] after N steps. 

Therefore, the plan is to compute:

- Compute the transition matrix T as above.

- Compute T raised to the Nth power, call it M.

- Apply this to the initial vector (which is 1 in state 0, 0 elsewhere) to get the final vector after N digits.

- Then compute the total as the sum over all vectors v (from 0 to 15) where F(P)=nonzero: which is when the XOR of the primes >=2 where v has 1's is not zero. 

So first, precompute for each vector v (0-15):

Check if the XOR of primes (2,3,5,7) which are set in the bits of v is non-zero. 

We can precompute a boolean array valid[v] which is true iff the XOR of their corresponding primes is non-zero.
